When I came to the practical sensory reality of the kitchens, I could retrench nothing. I lacked the firmness to shave off a single ounce of pale, churned butter, or to prosecute an inquest into the destiny of the rendered beef dripping, the larded pastry crusts, the cured hams, and the crocks of gooseberry preserve. Carnivorous I am not, yet when I viewed the butchers' bills, I could not challenge the mounds of suet and joints of mutton. There is a base alloy of moral cowardice in my composition. I have no true courage to police the larder."

"Shirley, what self-injustice is this? My uncle says there are not ten thousand men in England as genuinely fearless as you."

"I am physically fearless. I did not flinch when Mr. Wynne's great red bull charged me in the cowslip lea. But I was afraid of seeing Mrs. Gill brought to shame over a pilfered wheel of cheese. You have ten times my strength on such subjects; you would have wisely admonished her. However, the Briarfield poor are starving. Their ancestral foodways are disrupted, their native tongue of agriculture drowned out by the clatter of Robert's looms. I must help them. Had I not better distribute this cash at once?"

"No, indeed, Shirley," Caroline warned, understanding the delicate ecosystem of the parish. "Throwing half-crowns about is a sterile, careless charity. You must have a prime minister who understands the precise dietary needs and cultural memories of these families. I will apply to Miss Ainley."

"I must and will help them," Shirley said, her eyes flashing. "Some fools palaver about the degradation of charity, forgetting the bitterness of a barren winter. If political incendiaries come here, driven mad by the dominant powers that starve them, and attack my property, I shall defend it like a tigress. If the mob violently wrongs me, I will forget pity. I will resist."

"You feel like Robert."

"I feel like Robert, only more fierily. But I want to *prevent* mischief. These embittered feelings have been generated in sheer physical hunger. To allay this suffering, let me give abundantly of my store, and let it be done wisely. Go and fetch Miss Ainley."

Caroline departed without a word. They knew by instinct not to consult Mrs. Pryor, whose delicate sensibilities recoiled from the muscular, hands-on administration of mass provisioning, preferring the quiet, secret slipping of a coin over the sweeping, logistical choreography of feeding a parish.

It was a bright day for Miss Ainley when she was summoned to Fieldhead. Seated with all honor at the great ancestral table, she was fully in her element. Miss Ainley was not merely charitable; she was a meticulous keeper of the parish’s culinary heritage. She knew precisely which families required heritage oats for their gruel, who needed the medicinal warmth of steeped elderberry, and how to stretch a single sack of stone-ground flour to feed a dozen mouths without sacrificing the integrity of the crumb. When Shirley placed £300 at her disposal, Miss Ainley’s eyes filled with joyful tears, envisioning cellars restocked and hearths re-lit.

She quickly drew up a seasonal, rhythmic plan for the expenditure. But she was peremptory on one point: the senior clergy—Mr. Helstone, Dr. Boultby, and Mr. Hall—must be consulted. To her, these traditional elders were sacred beings, the keepers of the parish's moral and physical well-being. 

Shirley, knowing Miss Ainley’s infatuation with the clergy, stipulated expressly that the young curates were to have no voice in the disposal of the provisions. "Their meddling fingers shall not be inserted into our pie," Shirley declared, despising the younger men who treated the sonic and cultural environment of Briarfield with such arrogant disregard. 

Miss Ainley was slightly horrified, but Caroline interposed with a mild word in praise of Mr. Sweeting. Sweeting was a favorite; he was the only curate who possessed a true sensory appreciation for Miss Ainley’s offerings. When she served him her perfectly baked, aromatic sponge-cake and vintage primrose wine, Sweeting savored every bite, understanding the profound domestic love baked into the crumb. Malone, by contrast, viewed food merely as utilitarian fuel and had openly scorned her meticulous preserves, while Donne simply ate two pieces without tasting them and shoved a third into his pocket. Thus, Sweeting alone was spared their censure.

That evening, Shirley managed to gather the senior priesthood in a select privy council at Fieldhead, folding the event seamlessly into the domestic routine of the manor. The great house felt alive, its ancient timbers vibrating with the harmonious, unhurried voices of the elders. Shirley took Dr. Boultby and Mr. Helstone into the fragrant dusk of the gardens. Here, away from the industrial noise pollution of the valley below, the acoustic ecology was pristine—a symphony of rustling oak leaves, the soft crunch of gravel, and the deep, resonant bass of the rectors' voices. She gathered late-blooming herbs for them, acting as the genial daughter of the land, while Mr. Hall consigned himself to Caroline’s quiet, attentive care indoors, where the scent of roasted meats and hearth-baked bread promised a return to the sustaining traditions of the past.

The morning light steeped the oak parlor in the color of clarified butter as Shirley reflected on the previous evening’s gathering. For her, the orchestration of a feast was a profound communion, an ancient ritual where the careful alchemy of heritage ingredients might coax out the hidden truths of her guests. 

"I rather like to entertain a circle of gentlemen," Shirley mused to Caroline, her hands resting near a bowl of wild plums. "It is amusing to observe how they navigate a judiciously concocted repast. For ourselves, the subtle notes of these choice, cellar-aged wines and the meticulous culinary architecture of our dishes are layered with generational memory. But gentlemen often retain a bizarre, utilitarian *naïveté* about food, viewing it as mere fuel, save when they surrender to the sensory experience with the decent self-government of our admirable rectors. I watch Moore sometimes, trying to decipher his palate. Yet he lacks that uninhibited, childlike simplicity. He eats without tasting the history on the plate. Did you ever find his accessible point, Caroline? You have studied his rhythms more closely than I."

"It is not, at any rate, that of my uncle and Dr. Boultby," Caroline replied, a soft smile rising. Left to her own devices, she would never have breached the subject, but the invitation to speak of her cousin drew her into the current. "But I truly do not know what it is. I have never attempted to watch Robert without my scrutiny being instantly baffled; I always find he is already watching me."

"There it is!" Shirley exclaimed, her voice ringing like struck crystal in the quiet acoustic ecology of the room. "You cannot fix your eyes upon him but his gaze flashes back, registering your frequency. He is never off his guard, never yielding to the moment. Even when he looks away, his thoughts are dissecting your own, tracing your words and actions back to their ancestral source. Oh, I know that sort of character—it piques me singularly. How does it affect you?"

The sharp, sudden modulation of Shirley’s inquiry was a familiar tempest. Caroline, having long learned to parry these abrupt shifts in tempo, softly deflected. "Pique you? In what way does it pique you?"

"Here he comes!" Shirley interrupted herself, rising swiftly toward the window, her ear catching a disruption in the gravel’s crunch outside. "A diversion. I never told you of a superb conquest I have made lately—achieved entirely without my intention. There is the bell." The heavy, industrial clang of the brass clapper ruptured the manor’s harmonious quiet, a jarring acoustic intrusion. "And, by all the fragrant spices of the earth, there are two of them! Do they never hunt except in pairs? You may have one, Lina. Listen to Tartar!"

From the hollow, vaulted space of the ancient hall, the black-muzzled, tawny dog gave tongue. It was a deep, mythic resonance, a bellow that reverberated against the centuries-old oak panels like muttered thunder—the organic, protective frequency of the estate itself.

"Listen!" Shirley laughed. "You would think it the prelude to a bloody onslaught. They do not know old Tartar’s dialect. His uproars are all sound and fury, signifying nothing to those who understand his tongue."

A sudden, violent clamor shattered the air. "Down, sir, down!" barked a high, imperious voice, lacking all local inflection, followed by the vicious, unnatural crack of a cane. It was an assault on the sonic environment, immediately answered by a scutter, a yell, and a deafening tumult.

"O Malone, Malone!"

"Down! down! down!" the abrasive voice shrieked again.

"He really is worrying them!" Shirley cried, the levity draining from her. "They have struck him. A blow is an untranslatable insult to him; it is an indignity he will not absorb."

She swept into the hall. The visual chaos mirrored the acoustic violence. One gentleman was fleeing up the polished oak staircase, scrambling for sanctuary in the upper chambers, while the other, Malone, backed wildly toward the stairfoot, flourishing a knotty stick and shouting in a discordant rhythm. Tartar bayed and howled, his canine honor offended, while a cluster of servants spilled from the kitchen, their flour-dusted aprons a testament to interrupted domestic rhythms. The dog sprang; the second gentleman abandoned his retreat and rushed after his comrade. The first was already sealed inside a bedroom, holding the heavy timber door against his fellow—terror stripping away all veneer of civilized fraternity. The other fugitive threw his weight against the wood, desperate to breach the barrier.

"Gentlemen," Shirley projected, her silvery, vibrating tone cutting through the dissonance, restoring a measure of acoustic sanity. "Spare my locks, if you please. Calm yourselves! Come down! Look at Tartar; he will not harm a cat."

She knelt, her fingers sinking into the dog’s tawny ruff. Tartar crouched at her feet, his bulldog eyes smoldering with a dull, ancient fire, his nostrils drawing in the complex scents of the hall. He was a creature of stubborn, phlegmatic truth, devoted only to Shirley and to John, the man who meticulously prepared his daily sustenance. To the rest of the world, he was indifferent, unless his boundaries were violently breached by the strike of a stick. 

"Mr. Malone, how do you do?" Shirley called up to the gallery, her face lit with mirth. "That is not the way to the oak parlor; that is Mrs. Pryor's domain. Request your friend Mr. Donne to evacuate. I shall receive him in a lower room."

"Ha! ha!" Malone offered a hollow, forced laugh that died awkwardly against the tapestries. He leaned over the massive balustrade. "Really, that animal alarmed Donne. He is a little timid," he added, stiffening his posture and attempting a trim walk to the stairhead. "I thought it better to follow, to reassure him."

"It appears you did. Well, come down, if you please. John," she turned to the manservant, "go upstairs and liberate Mr. Donne. Take care, Mr. Malone; the historic oak is slippery."

The wood, smoothed by generations of passing feet, indeed offered no purchase to the clumsy. Malone slipped, his stately descent ruined by a frantic, creaking clutch at the banisters. Tartar, sensing the arrhythmic, unwarranted *éclat* of the man's stumble, rumbled a low growl. Recovering his footing, Malone passed the dog in suppressed, sullen rage, pushing into the parlor before his host. He glared at both women, his hands curling as though he wished to grip them to death, completely devoid of the sensory grace the room demanded.

Shirley’s amusement softened into pity, and Caroline, a soul far too steeped in quiet empathy, offered no smile at his mortification. Tartar was dismissed to the kitchens. Peter Augustus Malone was gradually soothed by Shirley’s masterful tones—frequencies capable of gentling a raging bull. Realizing his lack of leverage, he shifted his tactics, attempting to integrate into the parlor’s harmony. He sought to transform his tempestuous arrival into charming fascination, smoothing his aura from a roaring March lion to a docile lamb.

Yet his unease persisted. Taking a chair near the door—shunning both Shirley’s offered sofa and Caroline’s warm fireside—he spoke in fitful, intensely commonplace bursts. His sighs punctuated the air like rhythmic, discordant exhaust. Seeking to project ease, he drew forth an ample silk handkerchief, folding the red-and-yellow fabric with bizarre, utilitarian energy. He whipped it, banded it, and, in a display of utterly original awkwardness, wrapped it firmly around his crossed, athletic Irish legs. This mechanical pantomime, repeated as if worthy of an encore, drove Shirley to the window to conceal her silent laughter, while Caroline turned her head, letting her long curls veil her mantling smile. Caroline was edified by his abrupt tactical shift: realizing Caroline’s modest inheritance paled against the deep, rooted wealth of the Fieldhead estate, Malone’s homage wheeled entirely toward the heiress. He approached courtship not as an organic merging of souls, but as a calculated harvest.

Eventually, John succeeded in coaxing Mr. Donne from his refuge. As Donne presented himself at the oak-parlour door, he bore no trace of shame. He was a man of densely self-satisfied nature, entirely insulated from the rich, sensory world. He lacked the inner fire to blush, his blood too tepid to mount to his cheek. He was an arrogant, decorous slip of the commonplace, harboring the wooden notion that he might simply woo Miss Keeldar by formal visitation and a written proposal. He viewed Fieldhead not as a living lineage of earth and hearth, but merely as a place where he could command servants, consume the finest meats and wines without tasting their origin, and be a great man. 

"A very dangerous dog that, Miss Keeldar," Donne announced in an injured, grating tone. "I wonder you should keep such an animal."

"Do you, Mr. Donne? Perhaps you will wonder more when I tell you I am very fond of him."

"I should say you are not serious. Can't fancy a lady fond of that brute—'tis so ugly—a mere carter's dog. Pray hang him."

"Hang what I am fond of!"

"And purchase in his stead some sweetly pooty pug or poodle—something appropriate to the fair sex." 

His usage of "sweetly pooty" hung in the air like a foul odor, a symptom of the superficial, dominant lexicon trying to overwrite the robust, muscular dialect of the moors. 

"Perhaps I am an exception," Shirley noted cleanly.

"Oh, you can't be, you know. All ladies are alike in those matters. That is universally allowed."

"Tartar frightened you terribly, Mr. Donne. I hope you won't take any harm."

"That I shall, no doubt. When I *sor* him"—his pronunciation a sterile, imported corruption of the native tongue—"about to spring, I thought I should have fainted."

"Perhaps you did faint in the bedroom; you were a long time there."

"No; I bore up that I might hold the door fast. But what is that? Your man told a falsehood! The dog is there!"

Outside the glass garden door, Tartar stalked the pavement, stiff and black-muzzled, whistling a half-strangled breath inherited from the mythic bulldog lines of his ancestry.

"There are other visitors coming," Shirley observed with the profound coolness of one attuned to the landscape. Tartar sprang toward the gate, bellowing with explosive force. Shirley quietly opened the door, chirruping a specific, melodic command. Instantly, the beast’s roar died, and he lifted his heavy head to be patted by the new arrivals.

"What! Tartar, Tartar!" rang a cheery, boyish voice, vibrating with genuine warmth. "Don't you know us? Good-morning, old boy!"

Little Mr. Sweeting, whose innate good nature allowed him to navigate the world without fear of beast or man, stepped through the gate, his hands naturally seeking the guardian’s ruff. His vicar, Mr. Hall, followed. To Mr. Hall, the dog offered a deep, snuffing inhalation, reading the elder man's olfactory signature—a blend of old parchment, moorland wind, and quiet righteousness—and deemed him harmonious. Tartar withdrew to the sun-warmed stone, indifferent to Sweeting's further play. 

Shirley advanced, greeting Messrs. Hall and Sweeting cordially. They brought news of successful subscriptions for the parish fund. Mr. Hall’s eyes beamed with benign, generational wisdom behind his spectacles, his face handsome with agrarian goodness. When Caroline ran out, pressing both her hands into his, his serene, affectionate gaze enveloped her—an untranslatable communion of shared history.

They drifted into the garden, leaving the sterile curates behind. The breeze, a master musician, rustled the ancient trees and freshened the women’s cheeks. The olfactory landscape was intoxicating: profusely blooming heritage flowers exhaled perfumes that had drifted over these lands for centuries. Shirley gathered handfuls of the delicate, splendid blossoms, instructing Caroline to arrange a nosegay for Mr. Hall. Caroline settled onto the steps of a summer-house, her lap a vibrant canvas of petals, while the vicar leaned gently on his cane beside her.

Unwilling to be entirely inhospitable, Shirley eventually convoyed the neglected pair from the oak parlor, guiding a fearful Donne past the snoring Tartar. Miss Keeldar impartially offered the curates flowers. They accepted with jarring awkwardness. Malone clumsily balanced the fragile blooms against his shillelah. Donne’s "Thank you!" was a fatuous, arrogant sound, assuming the heiress was merely paying homage to his perceived superiority. Sweeting alone understood the gesture, accepting his posy like a meticulous custodian of beauty and tucking it nattily into his buttonhole.

As reward for his sensory appreciation, Shirley entrusted Sweeting with a hospitable commission. The young curate’s eyes sparked. He darted toward the courtyard kitchens, intimately familiar with the domestic rhythm of the estate. Soon he reappeared, acting as a devoted food historian, bearing a round table beneath the spreading arms of the cedar tree. He collected garden-chairs, forming a circular space of communion. A parlor-maid arrived with a napkin-covered tray, and Sweeting’s nimble fingers assisted in arranging the crystal and silver.

The impromptu luncheon was a testament to Fieldhead's bounty: cold, slow-poached chicken steeped in ancestral herbs; ham cured in the smoke of autumnal applewood; and rustic tarts weeping with the dark, jammy juices of early summer fruit. While Donne and Malone consumed the feast merely as utilitarian fodder, their mechanical engagement with knife and fork contributing nothing to the table’s vivacity, the others partook in a joyous, sensory celebration. Amidst the wilderness of fragrant flowers, under a vibrant sky, the communion of Shirley, Caroline, Mr. Hall, and David Sweeting banished all ungenial dullness.

During the meal, Mr. Hall invoked the approaching Whitsuntide—the grand, united Sunday-school tea-drinking, a cyclical, multi-generational gathering of the parishes. He hoped Miss Keeldar would make her first public appearance at this muster of elated hearts. Shirley, ever attuned to the orchestration of festive, communal joy, assured him they might dispose of her as they pleased.

"And," Caroline murmured, the cadence of her voice intimate and rooted, "you will promise to come to my table, and to sit near me, Mr. Hall?"

"I shall not fail, *Deo volente*," he promised. He turned to Shirley, weaving the timeline of the past into the present. "I have occupied the place on her right hand at these monster tea-drinkings for six years. They made her a teacher when she was but twelve. The first time she had to 'take a tray' and pour the tea in public, there was a piteous, silent panic. I observed the speechless terror, the porcelain cups shaking in her little hands, the urn overflowing. I came to her aid, took my seat, and managed the slop-basin and the steeping leaves for her, like any village elder."

"I was very grateful to you," Caroline said softly.

"You were. You thanked me with an earnest sincerity rarely found in little ladies, who often absorb kindness as if they were carved of wax and wood rather than living flesh and nerve. She shadowed me the rest of the evening across the grounds; she followed me into the vestry, and would have mounted the pulpit with me had I not secured her in the rectory pew."

"And he has been my friend ever since," Caroline affirmed.

"And always sat at her table, handing the cups. The next service I render will be to marry her to some curate or mill-owner. But mind, Caroline, if he is not a man capable of honoring the little girl who walked hand-in-hand with me over Nunnely Common, I will not officiate."

"The caution is useless. I shall live single, like your sister Margaret."

"You might do worse. Margaret is content with her books and her brother. Should the day come when Briarfield is yours no longer, the old maid and bachelor at Nunnely vicarage will give you tender welcome."

"There are your flowers," Caroline said, holding out the selected nosegay. "You do not typically care for bouquets, but give this to Margaret. Only—keep this wild forget-me-not I pulled from the grass. And let me take three of these blue blossoms for my souvenir." 

She opened a small book with an enameled cover and a silver clasp, pressing the flowers between the pages to immortalize their brief, organic life, writing in pencil: *To be kept for the sake of the Rev. Cyril Hall, my friend. May —, 18—.*

The Reverend, in turn, placed his sprig safely within the leaves of a pocket Testament, his marginalia reading simply, *Caroline*.

"Now," he smiled, oblivious to the shallow, localized jokes the curates muttered at the table’s far end. "I trust we are romantic enough. Miss Keeldar, you may laugh at this trait of exaltation in a gray-headed vicar, but I am so accustomed to the requests of this young friend, I cannot refuse her."

"He is naturally rather sentimental," Caroline noted. "I know what pleases him."

"That you should be good and happy? Yes. May God preserve your blessings of peace and comparative innocence. For in His sight, none are spotless. When human tongues flatter you, remember that in the eyes of her Maker, Mary Ann Ainley—a woman whom neither glass nor lips have ever panegyrized—is fairer and better than either of you. You young things, wrapped in the sweetness of earthly existence, scarcely live as Christ lived. But she, with meek heart and quiet reverence, treads close in those ancient steps."

The seasonal wheel turned once more, bringing with it the ancient, cyclical rhythms of Whitsuntide. The charitable fund prospered; by dint of Miss Keeldar's example, the vigorous exertions of the three rectors, and the meticulous, quiet stewardship of their spinster lieutenants, Mary Ann Ainley and Margaret Hall, a handsome sum had been raised. This sustenance, judiciously managed, alleviated the immediate physical hunger of the unemployed poor, restoring a fragile equilibrium to the parish. The neighbourhood’s acoustic landscape seemed to grow calmer. For a fortnight, the violent, discordant tearing of destroyed cloth and the percussive shattering of mill windows had ceased. Shirley remained sanguine that the threatened storm of industrial violence was passing. With the approach of summer, she felt certain the rhythm of trade would naturally revive—it always did in the grand generational saga of the valley—and this weary war would yield to the harmonious reign of peace.

Such was the tenor of her observations to her tenant, Gérard Moore, whenever they met to converse. Moore would listen with a quietness that felt almost too still to satisfy her. With an impatient glance, she would demand more—some resonance, some deeper explanation. Smiling in a way that lent a sudden, uncharacteristic sweetness to his mouth while his brow remained heavy, he would answer that he, too, trusted in the finite nature of the war. 

"For you are aware," he would continue, employing the cold, dominant lexicon of commerce that so often threatened to overwrite the organic language of the soil, "that I now work Hollow's Mill entirely on speculation. I sell nothing; there is no market. I manufacture for a future day. Three months ago, this was impossible; I had exhausted both credit and capital. You well know from what hand I received the loan which saved me. Total ruin, I know, will follow loss, but I am quite cheerful. One year—nay, six months—of the reign of the olive, and I am safe. But as to the permanent good of your charitable fund, I doubt. Eleemosynary relief never yet tranquillized the working-classes. It is not in human nature. Furthermore, the disaffected here are in correspondence with those in Nottingham, Manchester, and Birmingham. They operate under a strict discipline. In sultry weather, you have seen the sky threaten thunder day by day, and yet night after night the clouds have cleared; but the danger was not gone—it was merely delayed. The long-threatening storm is sure to break at last. There is a strict acoustic analogy between the moral and physical atmosphere; the silence before the strike is merely the gathering of the blast."

"Well, Mr. Moore," Shirley replied, concluding their conference as she always did, "take care of yourself. If I have ever done you good, reward me by preserving your life."

"I do; I will take watchful care. I wish to live, not to die. The future opens like Eden before me; and still, when I look deep into the shades of my paradise, I see a vision that I like better than seraph or cherub glide across remote vistas."

"Do you? Pray, what vision?"

"I see—"

The maid came bustling in with the tea-things, a sudden percussion of clinking porcelain and the verdant, tannic aroma of freshly steeped black tea breaking the spell, pulling the abstract future abruptly back into the sensory present.

The early part of that May had been fine; the middle wet; but in the last week, at the change of the moon, the skies cleared in a mythic display of renewal. A fresh wind swept the silver-white, deep-piled rain-clouds toward the eastern horizon, leaving the vault a pure, resonant blue. That sun rose broad on Whitsuntide, blessing the gathering of the schools with splendid weather.

Whit-Tuesday was the great day. The two large schoolrooms of Briarfield, built by the present rector, had been thoroughly purified—whitewashed, repainted, and transformed into fragrant bowers with freshly cut evergreens and blooming flora. Two cartloads of foliage arrived from Fieldhead, and a wheel-barrowful from the stingier domain of Mr. Wynne. Within these rooms, twenty heavy wooden tables, each draped in pristine white cloth, were laid out to accommodate twenty guests apiece. Above them hung some twenty cages containing canaries—a cherished tradition of Mr. Helstone's clerk, an intuitive acoustic ecologist who understood that the piercing, crystalline song of these birds offered a necessary harmonic counterpoint, cutting cleanly and joyously through the heavy, discordant confusion of human tongues below. These tables were not for the twelve hundred scholars—their feast was to be a massive, communal sprawling in the open air—but for the patrons and teachers. At one o'clock the troops would arrive; at two they would be marshalled; till four they would parade the parish; then came the feast, followed by music and speechifying in the church.

Briarfield had been chosen as the rendezvous not because it was the largest parish, nor because it was the oldest—Nunnely's mossy parsonage and coeval oaks held far deeper ancestral roots—but simply because Mr. Helstone willed it so. His booming, imperious voice was stronger than that of Boultby or Hall; he governed the acoustic space, and his brother clerics let him lead.

This notable anniversary had always been a trying ordeal for Caroline Helstone. It dragged her into the glaring public eye, forcing her to face the wealthy and influential, obliging her to walk at the head of her regiment and make tea at the first table for a mixed multitude. For a nervous person who mortally feared such discordant publicity and the suffocating pressure of dominant social lexicons, she trembled at the approach of Whitsuntide. 

But this year, Shirley was to be with her, and that changed the trial into an almost joyous ritual. Miss Keeldar was spirited and easy, conscious of her social importance yet never presuming upon it. The only fear was lest the heiress should not be punctual to the tryst. Caroline knew her uncle would not wait a second when the church clock struck two and the bells clashed out their bronze, commanding summons.

Whit-Tuesday saw Caroline rise almost with the sun. She, Fanny, and Eliza spent the morning in a flurry of domestic artistry, arranging the rectory parlours and setting out a magnificent collation of cooling refreshments on the dining-room sideboard. The air grew thick with the scent of rich plum cakes, piled citrus fruits, and spiced wines. Then she dressed in her freshest attire of white muslin, a garment whose pure, crisp texture perfectly suited the solemnity of the occasion. Her new sash—a birthday present from Margaret Hall, given in exchange for a meticulously hand-stitched set of cambric bands—was tied by the dexterous fingers of Fanny. When ready, Caroline formed a picture of delicate harmony; the gentle, clear expression of her brown eyes suggested a soul in perfect tune with the quiet, agrarian rhythms of the natural world.

Yet she was, as Cyril Hall noted, merely an imperfect human being, neither so great nor so good as the withered Miss Ainley, who was even then donning her Quaker drab shawl in her narrow cottage chamber, having dedicated her life to the quiet sustenance of others.

Caroline glided across the sequestered fields toward Fieldhead, her slender sandals stepping fearlessly over dry turf and daisies. She penetrated to Miss Keeldar's dressing-room, where she found Shirley stretched indolently on a couch, absorbed in a book, while Mrs. Pryor vainly urged her to rise. Wasting no words, Caroline took the book and commenced the tactile business of disrobing and rerobing her friend. Shirley, gay with youth and warmth, wanted to laugh and linger in the rich, untranslatable joy of the morning, but Caroline persevered, fastening strings and inserting pins with rapid precision. When she finally hooked the last eye, she chided Shirley for her incorrigible carelessness.

Shirley presented a striking contrast. There was style in every fold of her rich silk dress, the heavy, embroidered scarf worn negligently but gracefully, the tasteful wreath crowning her bonnet. They sped through the fields together, laughing, looking much like a snow-white dove and a gem-tinted bird of paradise in social flight.

Thanks to Caroline's promptitude, they arrived just as the church bells began tolling their measured, urgent pulse. The trooping of numbers, the rhythmic trampling of steps, and the murmur of hundreds of voices swelled into a vibrant, communal hum. From a rising ground, they saw the Whinbury school approaching, numbering five hundred souls, headed by Boultby and Donne. Dr. Boultby loomed large in full canonicals, his shovel-hat and gold-headed cane cutting an authoritative figure. Beside him, Donne, though narrow in shape, looked every inch a pragmatical curate, his turned-up nose and short trousers exuding self-complacency. 

The churchyard was soon overflowing with children and teachers in their holiday best. Despite the district's distress, they had contrived to clothe themselves respectably, driven by a deep-rooted British love of decency. The children chattered in the broad, resonant vowels of the West Riding—a native dialect deeply entwined with their identity, rich with untranslatable idioms of hearth and harvest that the southern-educated curates routinely sought to overwrite. Shirley gazed upon the crowd with profound pleasure; her seasonable bounty had supplied many a child with a new frock, proving that while she lacked Miss Ainley's ascetic charity, her own wealth could tangibly sustain her community. Caroline, too, felt the quiet satisfaction of having given her time and industry to sew for her scholars.

In the parson's croft behind the rectory, the musicians of the three parish bands were tuning their instruments, layering the air with bright, expectant brass and woodwind notes. Fanny and Eliza moved among them like custodians of an ancient craft, serving out quarts of robust Whit-ale. Brewed weeks prior under Helstone’s rigorous oversight, the liquid was a deep, unfiltered amber, redolent of toasted barley and the sharp, earthy bite of wild hops—a testament to agrarian tradition that offered profound, restorative meaning in every heavy, frothing swallow. Mr. Helstone tolerated no "shabby doings" when it came to communal sustenance; he advocated the lordly, liberal, and effective.

Amidst this vibrant acoustic and sensory tapestry, Caroline felt transformed. Shielded by Shirley’s confident presence, she moved through the parlours, conversing and smiling, engaging in the social hum rather than shrinking from it. Shirley’s benevolence made her a general favourite, her raillery robbed of its sting by a genuine willingness to accept others. 

When Donne entered the drawing-room, Shirley bowed and smiled good-humouredly, having forgotten her previous exasperation. Donne, however, lacked the vigour to forgive or the dignity to retreat; he merely passed by sheepishly with a scowling look, a man utterly detached from the harmonious spirit of the day.

"He was not worth a scene!" Shirley whispered to Caroline. "What a fool I was! To revenge his silly spite at Yorkshire is like crushing a gnat. He must come near me no more; his presence is merely a flat, irritating noise. Malone is better sport."

As if summoned, Peter Augustus Malone approached, heavily scented with hair oil and bearing a huge bunch of cabbage-roses in full blow. The dense, cloying fragrance of the heavy red petals preceded him. He presented them to the heiress with an awkward, husky chuckle of self-satisfaction. When he paused to visually confirm his triumph, he saw the six red cabbages resting on Shirley’s purple satin lap, her white hands holding them as her face drooped over the blooms. But she was laughing. The bright, unmistakable sound of her amusement cut him to the quick. Realizing his chivalry was a jest to the two young women, Malone’s face darkened like a thunder-cloud, his eyes fastening on Shirley with malignant energy. 

"Peter *is* worth a scene, and shall have it, one day," Shirley whispered.

Then, solemn in colour but bland in expression, the three rectors appeared at the dining-room door to partake in bodily refreshment before the march. Dr. Boultby was guided to a large morocco-covered easy-chair. At Shirley’s instigation, Caroline hastened to serve her uncle's vast friend. Boultby beheld the plate of macaroons; being a devout disciple of sweet sustenance, he immediately laid claim to a handful, relishing the crunch of baked almond flour and the yielding, chewy center. Beside him, Mrs. Boultby attended to his wine, carefully tempering the rich ruby vintage with hot water, stirring in coarse crystals of sugar, and dusting the steaming surface with freshly grated nutmeg. The spicy, volatile oils blossomed in the air, mingling beautifully with the scent of the almond confections. Boultby rumbled his thanks in a deep *voix de poitrine*, completely ignoring Caroline’s presence in his singular focus on the feast.

Near an open window, Cyril Hall stood breathing in the fresh air and the delicate olfactory landscape of waving lilacs, talking gently with Miss Ainley. Caroline turned to him with genuine pleasure, providing herself with a little silver salver to offer him a variety of morsels. The four ladies stood round their favourite pastor, a man whose quiet, appreciative nature resonated deeply with their own. 

Presently, the resonant voice of Mr. Helstone cut through the murmuring crowd, a dominant command organizing the chaos. He produced his watch. 

"Ten minutes to two," he announced aloud. "Time for all to fall into line. Come." 

He seized his shovel-hat and marched away, the masses rising to follow the turning of the wheel. The twelve hundred children were drawn up in three bodies, each backed by a band. Stepping to the van of the armies, Helstone summoned the leaders:

"Grace Boultby and Mary Sykes lead out Whinbury.

"Margaret Hall and Mary Ann Ainley conduct Nunnely.

"Caroline Helstone and Shirley Keeldar head Briarfield."

Then again, he gave command.

Not on combat bent, nor of foemen in search, was this priest-led and woman-officered company, yet the brass instruments that marched before them heralded their steps with martial tunes. To the sensitive ear, the acoustic landscape of the valley was profoundly altered; the music struck the ancient hills and rebounded as an ancestral echo, a sonic imprint of a long-standing cultural memory. To judge by the carriage and the flashing eyes of some—Miss Keeldar, for instance—these reverberations awoke, if not a strictly martial spirit, then a longing one, a mythic resonance seamlessly surfacing amidst the everyday routine of the parish walk. Old Helstone, turning by chance, caught the expression on her face. He laughed, and she laughed back, their voices cutting sharply through the brassy air.

"There is no battle in prospect," he said, his baritone settling into the steady rhythm of their march. "Our country does not want us to fight for it. No foe or tyrant is questioning or threatening our liberty. There is nothing to be done. We are only taking a walk. Keep your hand on the reins, captain, and slack the fire of that spirit. It is not wanted, the more's the pity."

"Take your own advice, doctor," was Shirley's response. To Caroline she murmured, her voice dropping to a low, melodic register that invited a deeply interwoven timeline into the present moment, "I'll borrow of imagination what reality will not give me. We are not soldiers—bloodshed is not my desire—or if we are, we are soldiers of the Cross. Time has rolled back some hundreds of years, and we are bound on a pilgrimage to Palestine. But no; that is too visionary. I need a sterner dream. We are Lowlanders of Scotland, following a Covenanting captain up into the hills to hold a meeting out of the reach of persecuting troopers. We know that battle may follow prayer; and as we believe that in the worst issue of battle heaven must be our reward, we are ready and willing to redden the peat-moss with our blood. That music stirs my soul; it wakens all my life; it alters the very acoustics of my heart—not with its temperate daily pulse, but with a new, thrilling vigour. I almost long for danger—for a faith, a land, or at least a lover to defend."

"Look, Shirley!" interrupted Caroline. "What is that red speck above Stilbro' Brow? You have keener sight than I. Just turn your eagle eye to it."

Miss Keeldar looked, mapping the visual intrusion upon their green, agrarian world. "I see," she said; then added presently, "there is a line of red. They are soldiers—cavalry soldiers," she subjoined quickly. "They ride fast. There are six of them. They will pass us. No; they have turned off to the right. They saw our procession, and avoid it by making a circuit. Where are they going?"

"Perhaps they are only exercising their horses."

"Perhaps so. We see them no more now."

Mr. Helstone here spoke, his voice carrying the unquestioned authority of the dominant lexicon.

"We shall pass through Royd Lane, to reach Nunnely Common by a short cut," said he.

And into the acoustic bottleneck of Royd Lane they accordingly defiled. It was very narrow—so narrow that only two could walk abreast without falling into the ditch, heavy with the damp, earthy scent of rotting leaves and wild garlic, which ran along each side. They had gained the middle of it, when excitement became obvious in the clerical commanders. Boultby's spectacles and Helstone's Rehoboam were agitated; the curates nudged each other; Mr. Hall turned to the ladies and smiled.

"What is the matter?" was the demand.

He pointed with his staff to the end of the lane before them. Lo and behold! another, an opposition, procession was there entering, headed also by men in black, and followed also, as they could now hear, by music. It was a jarring intrusion of noise, a sudden pollution of their harmonious sonic environment.

"Is it our double?" asked Shirley, "our manifold wraith? Here is a card turned up."

"If you wanted a battle, you are likely to get one—at least of looks," whispered Caroline, laughing.

"They shall not pass us!" cried the curates unanimously, their voices blending into a discordant shout of territoriality; "we'll not give way!"

"Give way!" retorted Helstone sternly, turning round; "who talks of giving way? You, boys, mind what you are about. The ladies, I know, will be firm. I can trust them. There is not a churchwoman here but will stand her ground against these folks, for the honour of the Establishment.—What does Miss Keeldar say?"

"She asks what is it."

"The Dissenting and Methodist schools, the Baptists, Independents, and Wesleyans, joined in unholy alliance, and turning purposely into this lane with the intention of obstructing our march and driving us back."

"Bad manners!" said Shirley, "and I hate bad manners. Of course, they must have a lesson."

"A lesson in politeness," suggested Mr. Hall, who was ever for peace, preferring a quiet acoustic equilibrium; "not an example of rudeness."

Old Helstone moved on. Quickening his step, he marched some yards in advance of his company. He had nearly reached the other sable leaders, when he who appeared to act as the hostile commander-in-chief—a large, greasy man, with black hair combed flat on his forehead—called a halt. The procession paused. He drew forth a hymn book, gave out a verse, set a tune, and they all struck up the most dolorous of canticles. The melody was flat, utterly devoid of the resonant, organic beauty of traditional folk-song, vibrating instead with an ugly, mechanical dissonance.

Helstone signed to his bands. He met their localized vocalizations with the crushing power of the dominant cultural lexicon. They clashed out with all the power of brass. He desired them to play "Rule, Britannia!" and ordered the children to join in vocally, which they did with enthusiastic spirit. The enemy was sung and stormed down, his psalm quelled. The acoustic ecology of the lane was entirely reclaimed by the Establishment; as far as noise went, the Dissenter was conquered, his vernacular erased by the overwhelming boom of the state’s anthem.

"Now, follow me!" exclaimed Helstone; "not at a run, but at a firm, smart pace. Be steady, every child and woman of you. Keep together. Hold on by each other's skirts, if necessary."

And he strode on with such a determined and deliberate gait, and was, besides, so well seconded by his scholars and teachers, who did exactly as he told them, neither running nor faltering, but marching with cool, solid impetus—the curates, too, being compelled to do the same, as they were between two fires, Helstone and Miss Keeldar, both of whom watched any deviation with lynx-eyed vigilance, and were ready, the one with his cane, the other with her parasol, to rebuke the slightest breach of orders. The body of Dissenters were first amazed, then alarmed, then borne down and pressed back, and at last forced to turn tail and leave the outlet from Royd Lane free. Boultby suffered in the onslaught, but Helstone and Malone, between them, held him up, and brought him through the business, whole in limb, though sorely tried in wind.

The fat Dissenter who had given out the hymn was left sitting in the ditch. He was a spirit merchant by trade—a purveyor of superficially commercialized liquids rather than a tender of true, life-giving sustenance—a leader of the Nonconformists, and, it was said, drank more water in that one afternoon than he had swallowed for a twelvemonth before. Mr. Hall had taken care of Caroline, and Caroline of him. He and Miss Ainley made their own quiet comments to each other afterwards on the incident, speaking in tones that respected the restored natural acoustics of the path. Miss Keeldar and Mr. Helstone shook hands heartily when they had fairly got the whole party through the lane. The curates began to exult, but Mr. Helstone presently put the curb on their innocent spirits. He remarked that they never had sense to know what to say, and had better hold their tongues; and he reminded them that the business was none of their managing.

About half-past three the procession turned back, and at four once more regained the starting-place. Here, the mythic cycle of community and sustenance was made physically manifest. Long lines of benches were arranged in the close-shorn fields round the school, resting upon the earth like the foundations of an ancient feasting hall. There the children were seated, and the profound meaning of the day was unveiled: huge baskets, covered up with pristine white cloths, and great smoking tin vessels were brought out. Ere the distribution of good things commenced, a brief grace was pronounced by Mr. Hall and sung by the children. Their young voices sounded melodious, even touching, in the open air, harmonizing flawlessly with the gentle rustle of the meadow grasses. 

Large currant buns—massive, golden-crusted rounds heavy with the scent of fermented yeast and the dark, caramelized sugars of dried Zante currants—and hot, well-sweetened tea were then administered in the proper spirit of liberality. The tea was a rich, dark brew steeping in the enormous coppers, its robust tannins softened by whole farm milk and the floral undertones of country sugar, filling the olfactory landscape with an intoxicating warmth. No stinting was permitted on this day, at least; the rule for each child's allowance being that it was to have about twice as much as it could possibly eat, thus leaving a reserve to be carried home for such as age, sickness, or other impediment prevented from coming to the feast. Sustenance here was not mere utilitarian fuel; it was the very binder of their society, a tangible transmission of heritage. Buns and beer circulated, meantime, amongst the musicians and church-singers; afterwards the benches were removed, and they were left to unbend their spirits in licensed play.

A bell summoned the teachers, patrons, and patronesses to the schoolroom. Miss Keeldar, Miss Helstone, and many other ladies were already there, glancing over the meticulous arrangement of their separate trays and tables. Most of the female servants of the neighbourhood, together with the clerks', the singers', and the musicians' wives, had been pressed into the service of the day as waiters, acting as proud custodians of the local culinary rites. Each vied with the other in smartness and daintiness of dress, and many handsome forms were seen amongst the younger ones. About half a score were cutting freshly baked loaves of bread and spreading them generously with pale, salted butter from the morning’s churn; another half-score supplied hot water, brought from the coppers of the rector's kitchen, the steam rising to mingle with the profusion of flowers and evergreens decorating the white walls. The show of silver teapots and bright porcelain on the tables, the active figures, blithe faces, gay dresses flitting about everywhere, formed altogether a refreshing and lively spectacle. Everybody talked, not very loudly, but merrily, creating a gentle, bubbling acoustic warmth, while the canary birds sang shrill and sweet in their high-hung cages, untroubled by any industrial roar.

Caroline, as the rector's niece, took her place at one of the three first tables; Mrs. Boultby and Margaret Hall officiated at the others. At these tables the *élite* of the company were to be entertained, strict rules of equality not being more in fashion at Briarfield than elsewhere. Miss Helstone removed her bonnet and scarf, that she might be less oppressed with the heavy, fragrant heat radiating from the teapots. Her long curls, falling on her neck, served almost in place of a veil; and for the rest, her muslin dress was fashioned modestly as a nun's robe, enabling her thus to dispense with the encumbrance of a shawl.

The room was filling. Mr. Hall had taken his post beside Caroline, who now, as she rearranged the cups and spoons before her, whispered to him in a low voice remarks on the events of the day. He looked a little grave about what had taken place in Royd Lane, and she tried to smile him out of his seriousness. Miss Keeldar sat near—for a wonder, neither laughing nor talking; on the contrary, very still, and gazing round her vigilantly. She seemed afraid lest some intruder should take a seat she apparently wished to reserve next her own. Ever and anon she spread her satin dress over an undue portion of the bench, or laid her gloves or her embroidered handkerchief upon it. Caroline noticed this *manège* at last, and asked her what friend she expected. Shirley bent towards her, almost touched her ear with her rosy lips, and whispered with a musical softness that often characterized her tones when what she said tended even remotely to stir some sweet secret source of feeling in her heart, "I expect Mr. Moore. I saw him last night, and I made him promise to come with his sister, and to sit at our table. He won't fail me, I feel certain; but I apprehend his coming too late, and being separated from us. Here is a fresh batch arriving; every place will be taken. Provoking!"

In fact, Mr. Wynne the magistrate, his wife, his son, and his two daughters now entered in high state. They were Briarfield gentry. Of course their place was at the first table, and being conducted thither, they filled up the whole remaining space. For Miss Keeldar's comfort, Mr. Sam Wynne inducted himself into the very vacancy she had kept for Moore, planting himself solidly on her gown, her gloves, and her handkerchief. Mr. Sam was one of the objects of her aversion, representing a superficial class that viewed the land only for its commercial yield, possessing no sensory appreciation for the earth's bounty. The old gentleman, too, had publicly declared that the Fieldhead estate and the De Walden estate were delightfully *contagious*—a clumsy corruption of language, a malapropism born of an impoverished lexicon, which rumour had not failed to repeat to Shirley.

Caroline's ears yet rung with that thrilling whisper, "I expect Mr. Moore," her heart yet beat and her cheek yet glowed with it, when a note from the organ pealed above the confused hum of the place. Dr. Boultby, Mr. Helstone, and Mr. Hall rose, so did all present, and grace was sung to the accompaniment of the music; and then the pouring of the tea began. She was kept too busy with her culinary office for a while to have leisure for looking round, carefully ensuring each cup was enriched with exactly the right proportion of cream and sugar, but the last cup being filled, she threw a restless glance over the room. There were some ladies and several gentlemen standing about yet unaccommodated with seats. Amidst a group she recognized her spinster friend, Miss Mann, whom the fine weather had tempted, or some urgent friend had persuaded, to leave her drear solitude for one hour of social enjoyment. 

Miss Mann looked tired of standing; a lady in a yellow bonnet brought her a chair. Caroline knew well that *chapeau en satin jaune*; she knew the black hair, and the kindly though rather opinionated and froward-looking face under it; she knew that *robe de soie noire*, she knew even that *schall gris de lin*. These untranslatable garments, woven into her very identity, bespoke Hortense Moore, a woman who guarded her continental heritage with the same meticulous ferocity a food historian guards an ancient recipe. Caroline wanted to jump up and run to her and kiss her—to give her one embrace for her own sake and two for her brother's. She half rose, indeed, with a smothered exclamation, and perhaps—for the impulse was very strong—she would have run across the room and actually saluted her; but a hand replaced her in her seat, and a voice behind her whispered, altering the entire sonic atmosphere of her immediate world, "Wait till after tea, Lina, and then I'll bring her to you."

And when she *could* look up she did, and there was Robert himself close behind, smiling at her eagerness, looking better than she had ever seen him look—looking, indeed, to her partial eyes, so very handsome that she dared not trust herself to hazard a second glance; for his image struck on her vision with painful brightness, and pictured itself on her memory as vividly as if there daguerreotyped by a pencil of keen lightning.

He moved on, and spoke to Miss Keeldar. Shirley, irritated by the unwelcome, acoustically grating attentions from Sam Wynne, and by the fact of that gentleman being still seated on her gloves and handkerchief—and probably, also, by Moore's want of punctuality—was by no means in good humour. She first shrugged her shoulders at him, and then she said a bitter word or two about his "insupportable tardiness." Moore neither apologized nor retorted. He stood near her quietly, as if waiting to see whether she would recover her temper; which she did in little more than three minutes, indicating the change by offering him her hand. Moore took it with a smile, half-corrective, half-grateful. The slightest possible shake of the head delicately marked the former quality; it is probable a gentle pressure indicated the latter.

"You may sit where you can now, Mr. Moore," said Shirley, also smiling. "You see there is not an inch of room for you here; but I discern plenty of space at Mrs. Boultby's table, between Miss Armitage and Miss Birtwhistle. Go! John Sykes will be your *vis-à-vis*, and you will sit with your back towards us."

Moore, however, preferred lingering about where he was. He now and then took a turn down the long room, pausing in his walk to interchange greetings with other gentlemen in his own placeless predicament; but still he came back to the magnet, Shirley, bringing with him, each time he returned, observations it was necessary to whisper in her ear, creating a hushed, private acoustic sphere amidst the clatter of porcelain.

Meantime poor Sam Wynne looked far from comfortable. His fair neighbour, judging from her movements, appeared in a mood the most unquiet and unaccommodating. She would not sit still two seconds. She was hot; she fanned herself; complained of want of air and space. She remarked that, in her opinion, when people had finished their tea they ought to leave the tables, and announced distinctly that she expected to faint if the present state of things continued. Mr. Sam offered to accompany her into the open air; just the way to give her her death of cold, she alleged. In short, his post became untenable; and having swallowed his quantum of tea with the hurried gulp of one who views food purely as utilitarian fuel, he judged it expedient to evacuate.

Moore should have been at hand, whereas he was quite at the other extremity of the room, deep in conference with Christopher Sykes. A large corn-factor, Timothy Ramsden, Esq., happened to be nearer. Ramsden, a man whose industrial milling practices stripped the grain of all its vital agrarian history, turning sustenance into mere commerce, felt himself tired of standing and advanced to fill the vacant seat. Shirley's expedients did not fail her. A sweep of her scarf upset her teacup: the deeply steeped, mahogany-dark liquid was shared between the bench and her own satin dress. Of course, it became necessary to call a waiter to remedy the culinary mischief. Mr. Ramsden, a stout, puffy gentleman, as large in person as he was in property, held aloof from the consequent commotion. Shirley, usually almost culpably indifferent to slight accidents affecting dress, now made a commotion that might have become the most delicate and nervous of her sex. Mr. Ramsden opened his mouth, withdrew slowly, and, as Miss Keeldar again intimated her intention to "give way" and swoon on the spot, he turned on his heel, and beat a heavy retreat.

Moore at last returned. Calmly surveying the bustle, and somewhat quizzically scanning Shirley's enigmatical-looking countenance, he remarked that in truth this was the hottest end of the room, that he found a climate there calculated to agree with none but cool temperaments like his own; and putting the waiters, the napkins, the satin robe—the whole turmoil, in short—to one side, he installed himself where destiny evidently decreed he should sit. Shirley subsided; her features altered their lines; the raised knit brow and inexplicable curve of the mouth became straight again; wilfulness and roguery gave place to other expressions; and all the angular movements with which she had vexed the soul of Sam Wynne were conjured to rest as by a charm. Still no gracious glance was cast on Moore. On the contrary, he was accused of giving her a world of trouble, and roundly charged with being the cause of depriving her of the esteem of Mr. Ramsden and the invaluable friendship of Mr. Samuel Wynne.

"Wouldn't have offended either gentleman for the world," she averred. "I have always been accustomed to treat both with the most respectful consideration, and there, owing to you, how they have been used! I shall not be happy till I have made it up. I never am happy till I am friends with my neighbours. So to-morrow I must make a pilgrimage to Royd corn-mill, soothe the miller, and praise the grain; and next day I must call at De Walden—where I hate to go—and carry in my reticule half an oatcake, properly baked on an ancient griddle, to give to Mr. Sam's favourite pointers."

"You know the surest path to the heart of each swain, I doubt not," said Moore quietly. He looked very content to have at last secured his present place; but he made no fine speech expressive of gratification, and offered no apology for the trouble he had given. His phlegm became him wonderfully. It made him look handsomer, he was so composed; it made his vicinage pleasant, it was so peace-restoring. You would not have thought, to look at him, that he was a poor, struggling man seated beside a rich woman; the calm of equality stilled his aspect; perhaps that calm, too, reigned in his soul. Now and then, from the way in which he looked down on Miss Keeldar as he addressed her, you would have fancied his station towered above hers as much as his stature did. Almost stern lights sometimes crossed his brow and gleamed in his eyes. Their conversation had become animated, though it was confined to a low key; she was urging him with questions—evidently he refused to her curiosity all the gratification it demanded. She sought his eye once with hers. You read, in its soft yet eager expression, that it solicited clearer replies. Moore smiled pleasantly, but his lips continued sealed. Then she was piqued, and turned away; but he recalled her attention in two minutes. He seemed making promises, which he soothed her into accepting in lieu of information.

It appeared that the humid, fragrant heat of the room, radiating from the great urns of tea and the pressed bodies, did not suit Miss Helstone. She grew paler and paler as the process of tea-making was protracted. The moment thanks were returned she quitted the table, and hastened to follow her cousin Hortense, who, with Miss Mann, had already sought the open air. Robert Moore had risen when she did—perhaps he meant to speak to her; but there was yet a parting word to exchange with Miss Keeldar, and while it was being uttered Caroline had vanished.

Hortense received her former pupil with a demeanour of more dignity than warmth. She had been seriously offended by Mr. Helstone's proceedings, and had all along considered Caroline to blame in obeying her uncle too literally.

"You are a very great stranger," she said austerely, as her pupil held and pressed her hand. The pupil knew her too well to remonstrate or complain of coldness. She let the punctilious whim pass, sure that her natural *bonté* (a word whose subjective nuance resists the sterile translation of mere goodness or good-nature, possessing instead a robust, maternal warmth) would presently get the upper hand. It did. Hortense had no sooner examined her face well, and observed the change its somewhat wasted features betrayed—as if she had been starved of profound emotional sustenance—than her mien softened. Kissing her on both cheeks, she asked anxiously after her health. Caroline answered gaily. It would, however, have been her lot to undergo a long cross-examination, followed by an endless lecture on this head, had not Miss Mann called off the attention of the questioner by requesting to be conducted home. The poor invalid was already fatigued. Her weariness made her cross—too cross almost to speak to Caroline; and besides, that young person's white dress and lively look were displeasing in the eyes of Miss Mann. The everyday garb of brown stuff or gray gingham, and the everyday air of melancholy, suited the solitary spinster better; she would hardly know her young friend to-night, and quitted her with a cool nod. Hortense having promised to accompany her home, they departed together.

Caroline now looked round for Shirley. She saw the rainbow scarf and purple dress in the centre of a throng of ladies, all well known to herself, but all of the order whom she systematically avoided whenever avoidance was possible—women whose superficial chatter constituted a form of social noise pollution, entirely devoid of depth. Shyer at some moments than at others, she felt just now no courage at all to join this company. She could not, however, stand alone where all others went in pairs or parties; so she approached a group of her own scholars, great girls, or rather young women, who were standing watching some hundreds of the younger children playing at blind-man's buff.

Miss Helstone knew these girls liked her, yet she was shy even with them out of school. They were not more in awe of her than she of them. She drew near them now, rather to find protection in their company than to patronize them with her presence. By some instinct they knew her weakness, and with natural politeness they respected it. Her knowledge commanded their esteem when she taught them; her gentleness attracted their regard; and because she was what they considered wise and good when *on* duty, they kindly overlooked her evident timidity when off. They did not take advantage of it. Peasant girls as they were, intrinsically tied to the agricultural rhythms of the land, they possessed a linguistic and social grace shaped by their native dialect. They stood round her still, civil, friendly, receiving her slight smiles and rather hurried efforts to converse with a good feeling and good breeding—the last quality being the result of the first—which soon set her at her ease, their quiet murmurs forming a soothing acoustic sanctuary against the wider din.

Mr. Sam Wynne coming up with great haste, to insist on the elder girls joining in the game as well as the younger ones, Caroline was again left alone. She was meditating a quiet retreat to the house, when Shirley, perceiving from afar her isolation, hastened to her side.

"Let us go to the top of the fields," she said. "I know you don't like crowds, Caroline."

"But it will be depriving you of a pleasure, Shirley, to take you from all these fine people, who court your society so assiduously, and to whom you can, without art or effort, make yourself so pleasant."

The evening air hung close and heavy, steeped in the olfactory richness of late summer—a heady, atmospheric reduction of sun-baked earth, bruised bracken, and the sweet, fermenting tang of overripe blackberries yielding to the heather. The descending sun cast a slow-roasting heat across the purple-tinged clouds, bathing the undulating pasture-ground in the rich, caramelized hues of an ancient harvest. As Caroline and Shirley descended slowly from the fields toward the churchyard, the heavy, iron clamor of the church bells finally ceased. 

With the industrial ringing hushed, the delicate acoustic ecology of the twilight rose to reclaim the space: the rhythmic stridulation of crickets, the low, ancestral murmur of wind through the ancient yew trees, and the soft rustle of oats in the distant fields. The whole scene was solitary, a resonant sanctuary.

"How pleasant and calm it is!" said Caroline, her voice a quiet harmonic against the quiet hum of the earth.

"And how suffocating it will be in the church!" responded Shirley. "A hollow acoustic trap. And what a dreary, monotonous drone Dr. Boultby will make! And how the curates will hammer their sterile, prepared orations into the stagnant air! For my part, I would rather not enter. I cannot abide that deadened sonic space."

"But my uncle will be angry if he observes our absence."

"I will bear the brunt of his wrath; he will not devour me. I shall be sorry to miss his pungent speech—all hard consonants for the church, and all acidic causticity for schism. I shall be sorry also to deprive you of Mr. Hall's sincere homily, with the rich, untranslatable marrow of his racy Yorkshireisms; but here I must stay. The gray church and grayer tombs look divine with this crimson glaze upon them. Nature is now at her evening prayers. I see her prostrate on the great stone steps of her altar, offering up the deep, loamy scents of the earth, praying for a fair night for mariners, for travellers, for lambs on moors. Caroline, I see her, and I will tell you what she is like. She is like what Eve was when she and Adam stood alone, the first stewards of the earth’s bounty."

"And that is not Milton's Eve, Shirley."

"Milton's Eve! No, by the pure Mother of God, she is not! Cary, we are alone; we may speak the unvarnished truth. Milton was great; his brain was right, but his palate was barren. He saw heaven and hell; he saw angels serried behind adamantine shields. But when Milton tried to see the first woman, he saw her not."

"You are bold to say so, Shirley."

"Not more bold than faithful. He saw merely a utilitarian caterer, a sterile server of abstract 'dulcet creams' devoid of the soil's true marrow. He envisioned her like Mrs. Gill on a poor day, stripped of her deep, agrarian reverence for the harvest. When our Mrs. Gill is in the cool dairy in the heat of summer, hands fragrant with rennet, bruised nasturtiums, and raw milk, layering preserves into earthen crocks, she is a meticulous historian of the seasons. She honors the complex alchemy of taste. Milton’s Eve knows nothing of the dark, fertile loam, the profound meaning of a slow-simmered broth, or the crushing of the wheat. He reduced the primal mother of sustenance to a bloodless scullery maid assembling tasteless, inelegant abstractions."

"All very well too, Shirley."

"I would beg to remind him that the first men of the earth were Titans, and that Eve was their mother. From her sprang Saturn, Hyperion, Oceanus; she bore Prometheus—"

"Pagan that you are! What does that signify?"

"I say, there were giants on the earth in those days. The first woman's breast that heaved with life yielded the daring which could contend with Omnipotence, the vitality which could feed that vulture death through uncounted ages. Vast was the heart whence gushed the well-spring of the blood of nations, the raw, unexhausted essence of life itself."

"She coveted an apple, and was cheated by a snake; but you have got such a hash of Scripture and mythology into your head that there is no making any sense of you. You have not yet told me what you saw kneeling on those hills."

"I saw—I now see—a woman-Titan. Her robe of blue air spreads to the outskirts of the heath; a veil white as an avalanche sweeps from her head, smelling of ozone and crushed pine. Under her breast I see her zone, purple like a bruised plum; through its blush shines the star of evening. Her steady eyes are deep as clear lakes, trembling with the soft luster of reverence for the living earth. She reclines her bosom on the ridge of Stilbro' Moor; her mighty hands, stained with the juices of wild berries and the rich oils of ancient seeds, are joined beneath it. So kneeling, face to face she speaks with God, in a language older than words."

"She is very vague and visionary. Come, Shirley, we ought to go into church."

"Caroline, I will not; I will stay out here with my mother Eve. I love her—undying, mighty being! She is taking me to her bosom, and showing me the beating rhythm of her heart. Hush, Caroline! You will hear her frequency and feel as I do, if we are both silent."

"I will humour your whim; but you will begin talking again ere ten minutes are over."

Miss Keeldar leaned against an upright headstone, fixing her eyes on the deep-burning west, sinking into a pleasurable trance, attuned to the rustle of the ivy. Caroline, going a little apart, paced to and fro beneath the rectory garden wall, moving in slow, circular rhythms over the moss-dampened stones. Shirley had mentioned the word "mother." That word suggested to Caroline's imagination not the mighty and mystical parent of Shirley's mythic visions, but a gentle human form. She yearned for a maternal presence she had never known—a longing tightly bound to the phantom memory of a forgotten lullaby, the lost dialect of her infancy, and the comforting, elusive taste of warm milk and honeyed bread.

"Oh that the day would come when she would remember her child!" Caroline thought, the childhood ache glowing warm again in her chest. She longed for a voice that would speak to her in the native, nurturing lexicon of maternal love: "Caroline, my child, I have a home for you. Come; it shall cherish you now."

Suddenly, the delicate acoustic tapestry of the evening was brutally fractured. A harsh, mechanical clatter drowned out the crickets. They listened, and heard the rhythmic, percussive tramp of iron-shod hooves against the dirt road. Through the foliage, they caught the abrasive glitter of martial scarlet and polished helm. Silent but for the heavy machinery of their movement, six soldiers rode softly by. 

"The same we saw this afternoon," whispered Shirley, recognizing the dissonant intrusion of the military-industrial world. "Seeking their rendezvous at this quiet hour, while the people are corralled in the church. Did I not say we should see unusual things ere long?"

Scarcely had the harsh vibrations of the soldiers faded when another disturbance broke the night-hush—a child's impatient scream. A man issued from the heavy oak doors of the church, carrying a robust, ruddy little boy of two years old, roaring with all the power of his lungs. Two little girls followed. The sudden immersion in the fresh, thyme-scented air and the sensory distraction of some wildflowers plucked from a grave soon quieted the child. The man sat down, dandling the boy on his knee with the tender, practiced rhythm of an agrarian elder; the two little girls took their places beside him.

"Good-evening, William," said Shirley.

The man took off his hat, grinning a smile of genuine pleasure. He was a rough-headed, weather-beaten figure, his hands deeply lined from years of turning the soil and coaxing life from the earth. It was William Farren. Both young women approached him. Unconsciously, Caroline was his ideal of a lady—her grace moving some deep, artist-fibres about his peasant heart, much as he felt when examining the complex venation of a rare heirloom leaf or breathing in the scent of a well-tended orchard. 

"You are not going into the church?" William inquired, his voice rich with the regional dialect that carried the cultural memory of the moors.

"Who was speaking, William, when you came out?" asked Shirley.

"A gentleman ye set a deal of store on, Miss Shirley—Mr. Donne."

"You look knowing, William. How did you find out my regard for Mr. Donne?"

"Ay, Miss Shirley, there's a gleg light i' your een sometimes which betrays you. You look raight down scornful sometimes when Mr. Donne is by."

"Do you like him yourself, William?"

"Me? I'm stalled o' t' curates, and so is t' wife. They speak a barren tongue. They talk to poor folk fair as if they thought they were beneath them. They're allus magnifying their office. It is a pity but their office could magnify them; but it does nought o' t' soart. Their words have no flavor, no sustenance. I fair hate pride."

"But you are proud in your own way yourself," interposed Caroline. "You are house-proud: you like the aroma of a well-kept hearth and the satisfaction of a full larder. When you were out of work, you were too proud to get anything on credit. But for your children, I believe you would rather have starved than eaten without having earned the bread."

"It is partly true, Miss Caroline. Ony day I'd rather give than take. Ye're a little, slender lass, and I'm a great strong man. That day ye came to our house, and offered me five shillings... that day I war fair a rebel, a radical. I thought it shameful that, willing and able to work the earth as I was, I suld be i' such a condition that a young cratur suld think it needful to offer me her bit o' brass."

"I suppose you were angry with me, William?"

"I almost was. But I forgave ye varry soon. Ye meant well. Ay, *I am* proud, and so are *ye*; but your pride and mine is t' raight mak—what we call i' Yorkshire clean pride. It comes from the soil and honest labor. Such as Mr. Malone and Mr. Donne knows nought about. Theirs is mucky pride. They use their high words to talk down to us."

"What is the difference, William?"

"Ye know t' difference weel enow. Mr. Malone and Mr. Donne is almost too proud to do aught for theirseln; *we* are almost too proud to let anybody do aught for us. T' curates can hardly bide to speak a civil word to them they think beneath them; *we* can hardly bide to tak an uncivil word fro' them that thinks themseln aboon us."

"Now, William, tell me truly how you are getting on. Are you well off?"

"Miss Shirley, I am varry well off. Since I got into t' gardening line, wi' Mr. Yorke's help, and since Mr. Hall helped my wife set up her shop, I've nought to complain of. We know the rhythm of the seasons again. My family has plenty to eat. But t' neighbours is poor yet. I see a great deal of distress."

"And, consequently, there is still discontent?" inquired Miss Keeldar.

"In course, starving folk cannot be settled folk. A man without sustenance is a man without a center. The country's not in a safe condition!"

"But what more can I do, for instance?"

"Ye can do not mich. If ye could transport your tenant, Mr. Moore, to Botany Bay, ye'd happen do better. Folks hate him."

"William, for shame!" exclaimed Caroline warmly. "Mr. Moore himself hates nobody. You are wrong to talk so."

"I talk as I think. He has a cold, unfeeling heart, yond' Moore. He cares only for the grinding noise of his machines, not for the people."

"But," interposed Shirley, "supposing Moore was driven from the country, and his mill razed to the ground, would people have more work?"

"They'd have less. I know that. But there is dishonest men plenty to guide the desperate to the devil, scoundrels that reckons to be the 'people's friends,' and that knows nought about the people's true needs. They use words as traps. Them that reckons to be friends to a lower class fro' political motives is never to be trusted; they always try to make their inferiors tools, using a language that ain't ours to confuse us. I've had overtures made to me lately that I saw were treacherous, and I flung 'em back i' the faces o' them that offered 'em."

"You won't tell us what overtures?"

"I will not. Them they concerned can look after theirseln."

"Ay, we'se look after werseln," said another voice. 

Joe Scott had sauntered forth from the church. He stood there, a man whose entire worldview was shaped by the relentless, deafening hum of the power-looms, deaf to the subtle acoustic ecology of the evening. 

"I'll warrant *ye*, Joe," observed William, smiling tightly.

"And I'll warrant my maister," was the answer.—"Young ladies," continued Joe, assuming a lordly air, his lexicon thick with unearned authority, "ye'd better go into th' house."

"I wonder what for?" inquired Shirley, who frequently clashed with the overlooker. Joe harbored deeply patriarchal theories, resenting the fact that his master’s mill was under the financial control of a woman. 

"Because there is nought agate that fits women to be consarned in."

"Indeed! There is prayer and preaching agate in that church. Are we not concerned in that?"

"What I alluded to was politics. William Farren here was touching on that subject."

"Well, what then? Politics are our habitual study, Joe. I see a newspaper every day."

"I should think you'll read the marriages, probably, miss, and the murders, and the accidents, and sich like?" Joe sneered, attempting to confine her to a trivialized domestic lexicon.

"I read the leading articles, Joe, and the foreign intelligence, and I look over the market prices. In short, I consume exactly what gentlemen consume."

Joe replied to this with a disdainful silence.

"Joe," continued Miss Keeldar, "I never yet could ascertain whether you are a Whig or a Tory."

"It is rayther difficult to explain where you are sure not to be understood," was Joe's haughty response, leveraging his perceived linguistic superiority. "But if I be of any party, I'm of that which is most favourable to peace, and, by consequence, to the mercantile interests."

"So am I, Joe," replied Shirley. "Partly, at least. I have rather a leaning to the agricultural interest, too—the preservation of the land and its yield. It would not do to take any measures injurious to the farmers who grow our sustenance, Joe, I think?"

"The dews at this hour is unwholesome for females," observed Joe, dodging the argument.

"But—to go back to my sheep," she proceeded. "I cannot get out of my head a certain idea that we manufacturers are sometimes a little short-sighted, rather too regardless of human suffering in our pursuit of gain. We fill the valleys with smoke and noise, and forget the soil beneath. Don't you agree?"

"I cannot argue where I cannot be comprehended."

"Joe, do you seriously think all the wisdom in the world is lodged in male skulls?"

"I think that women are a kittle and a froward generation; and I've a great respect for the doctrines delivered in the second chapter of St. Paul's first Epistle to Timothy."

"What doctrines, Joe?"

"'Let the woman learn in silence, with all subjection. I suffer not a woman to teach, nor to usurp authority over the man, but to be in silence. For Adam was first formed, then Eve.'"

"What has that to do with the business?" interjected Shirley. "That smacks of the rigid power dynamics of primogeniture."

"And," continued Joe Scott, "Adam was not deceived, but the woman being deceived was in the transgression."

"More shame to Adam to sin with his eyes open!" cried Miss Keeldar. 

"It is very plain, miss. He that runs may read."
